<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $\Lambda$ be a finite nonempty alphabet, and let $$\Phi=(\varphi_i)_{i\in\Lambda},\qquad
 \varphi_i(x)=r_i x+t_i,\qquad t_i\in\mathbb R,\quad 0<|r_i|<1.$$ The family is indexed: different symbols may specify the same map. Given a probability vector $p=(p_i)_{i\in\Lambda}$ with $p_i>0$, its self-similar measure is the unique Borel probability measure satisfying $$\mu=\sum_{i\in\Lambda}p_i(\varphi_i)_*\mu.$$ Equivalently, $\mu$ is the law of $\lim_{n\to\infty}\varphi_{I_1}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{I_n}(0)$, where the symbols $I_j$ are independent with law $p$. The limit exists uniformly in the address: if $r_{\max}=\max_i|r_i|$ and $T_0=\max_i|t_i|$, all coding limits have absolute value at most $T_0/(1-r_{\max})$.

For a word $w=i_1\cdots i_n$, write $\varphi_w=\varphi_{i_1}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{i_n}$ and $p_w=p_{i_1}\cdots p_{i_n}$. An *exact overlap* is an equality $\varphi_u=\varphi_v$ for distinct words of the same positive length. Equality here means equality of the complete affine maps, including both the signed slope and the translation. We allow all such overlaps.

The relevant entropy counts maps rather than addresses. Set $$G_n=\varphi_{I_1}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{I_n},\qquad
 \mathbb P(G_n=g)=\sum_{w\in\Lambda^n:\,\varphi_w=g}p_w.$$ All logarithms below have base two. For a finite-valued random variable $Z$, its Shannon entropy is $H(Z)=-\sum_z\mathbb P(Z=z)\log\mathbb P(Z=z)$, with $0\log0=0$. Define the random-walk entropy rate and the Lyapunov exponent by <a id="eq:rate-and-lyapunov"></a>$$\label{eq:rate-and-lyapunov}
 h=h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\Phi,p)
   :=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{H(G_n)}n
    =\inf_{n\ge1}\frac{H(G_n)}n,
 \qquad
 \chi=-\sum_i p_i\log|r_i|>0.$$ Indeed, a length-$(n+m)$ map is a function of two independent maps with laws $G_n,G_m$, so $H(G_{n+m})\le H(G_n)+H(G_m)$; subadditivity gives the displayed limit and infimum. Also $0\le h\le H(p):=-\sum_i p_i\log p_i$. The ratio $h/\chi$ is independent of the common logarithm base.

We use the lower Hausdorff dimension of a measure: $$\dim_H\nu=\inf\{\dim_H E:E\subset\mathbb R\text{ Borel},\ \nu(E)>0\}.$$ Feng–Hu’s exact-dimensionality theorem (Feng and Hu 2009, Theorem 2.8) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[FengHu2009](#ref-FengHu2009)] applies to finite self-similar systems without separation. Indeed, the maps preserve a sufficiently large compact interval, extend to contracting smooth diffeomorphisms, and have derivative norm and least singular value both equal to $|r_i|$. The system is therefore conformal, and its Bernoulli coding law is ergodic. These observations also cover negative ratios and repeated indexed maps. Thus, for the measure $\mu$ above, there is a constant $d$ such that <a id="eq:exact-dimensionality"></a>$$\label{eq:exact-dimensionality}
 \lim_{s\downarrow0}\frac{\log\mu(B(x,s))}{\log s}=d
 \quad\text{for $\mu$-almost every $x$}.$$ For an exact-dimensional measure this constant equals both $\dim_H\mu$ and $\inf\{\dim_H E:\mu(E)=1\}$. To see the equivalence, restrict to countably many sets on which the local bounds $s^{d+\varepsilon}\le\mu(B(x,s))\le s^{d-\varepsilon}$ hold uniformly for all sufficiently small $s$. The lower mass bound gives a full-measure union of sets of dimension at most $d+\varepsilon$ by a covering argument; the upper mass bound gives dimension at least $d-\varepsilon$ for every positive-mass set. Let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. Consequently either Hausdorff measure-dimension convention gives the same statement below.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every finite nonempty indexed family $\varphi_i(x)=r_i x+t_i$ on $\mathbb R$ with $0<|r_i|<1$, and every strictly positive probability vector $p$, its self-similar measure satisfies $$\dim_H\mu_{\Phi,p}
 =\min\left\{1,\frac{h_{\mathrm{RW}}(\Phi,p)}{\chi(\Phi,p)}\right\}.$$ No separation assumption is required; exact overlaps and repeated generators are allowed.*

</div>

This proves the entropy-rate dimension conjecture, formulated as Conjecture 3 in Varjú’s survey (Varjú 2026) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[VarjuSurvey2026](#ref-VarjuSurvey2026)]. When there are no exact overlaps, $H(G_n)=nH(p)$, and the theorem gives the usual entropy-to-Lyapunov formula. Section <a href="#sec:corollaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:corollaries">6</a> records this case, the attractor formula, and the homogeneous all-weight consequence, including the three-map family $\{\lambda x,\lambda x+1,\lambda x+t\}$. The theorem includes critical and supercritical entropy rates. It asserts dimension, without an assertion of absolute continuity.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Background and proof strategy

The dimension of a self-similar set is classical when its pieces are sufficiently separated. Moran’s construction (Moran 1946) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Moran1946](#ref-Moran1946)] and Hutchinson’s iterated-function-system framework (Hutchinson 1981) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Hutchinson1981](#ref-Hutchinson1981)] give the similarity-dimension formula under the open set condition. Hutchinson also established the invariant-measure construction used above (Hutchinson 1981, Theorem 4.4(1)) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Hutchinson1981](#ref-Hutchinson1981)]. Removing separation leads to the exact-overlap problem: can dimension fall below its natural upper bound when distinct words never define the same map? The set version is associated with Simon (Simon 1996) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Simon1996](#ref-Simon1996)]; see also the formulation and historical discussion in (Varjú 2026, sec. 1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[VarjuSurvey2026](#ref-VarjuSurvey2026)]. The entropy-rate formulation addresses the additional loss of information when exact overlaps do occur.

Bernoulli convolutions provide an early and influential model for this question. These are the laws of $\sum_{j\ge0}\varepsilon_j\lambda^j$, where $0<\lambda<1$ and the $\varepsilon_j$ are independent equiprobable signs. Erdős proved singularity for reciprocal Pisot parameters in $(1/2,1)$ (Erdős 1939) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Erdos1939](#ref-Erdos1939)]. A Pisot number is a real algebraic integer greater than one whose other conjugates have modulus less than one. Solomyak proved absolute continuity with an $L^2$ density for almost every parameter in $(1/2,1)$ (Solomyak 1995) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Solomyak1995](#ref-Solomyak1995)]; Peres–Solomyak later gave a simpler proof (Peres and Solomyak 1996) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[PeresSolomyak1996](#ref-PeresSolomyak1996)]. Garsia introduced the discrete entropy rate in this setting and related it to singularity (Garsia 1963, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Garsia1963](#ref-Garsia1963)]. These results illustrate the sensitivity to overlaps and arithmetic; absolute continuity and Hausdorff dimension are distinct questions.

Hochman showed that $\dim_H\mu<\min\{1,H(p)/\chi\}$ forces superexponential concentration of cylinder maps (Hochman 2014, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Hochman2014](#ref-Hochman2014)], using inverse theorems for entropy growth under convolution. For unequal ratios his entropy formulation already keeps track of the full affine map, including its slope (Hochman 2014, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Hochman2014](#ref-Hochman2014)]. The entropy-rate formula under weak exponential separation follows from his work; the version allowing exact collisions is proved in Bárány–Verma (Bárány and Verma 2026, Theorem 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BaranyVerma2026](#ref-BaranyVerma2026)]. For Bernoulli convolutions, Breuillard–Varjú developed approximation by algebraic parameters with controlled entropy (Breuillard and Varjú 2019) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BreuillardVarju2019](#ref-BreuillardVarju2019)]. Building on these and earlier entropy methods, Varjú proved full dimension for every transcendental parameter in $(1/2,1)$ (Varjú 2019b, Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[VarjuTranscendental2019](#ref-VarjuTranscendental2019)]. Rapaport proved the no-exact-overlap formula for algebraic contraction ratios and arbitrary real translations, allowing signed unequal ratios (Rapaport 2022, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Rapaport2022](#ref-Rapaport2022)].

Rapaport–Varjú extended entropy and approximation methods to homogeneous three-map systems (Rapaport and Varjú 2024) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[RapaportVarju2024](#ref-RapaportVarju2024)]. For equal weights, their results include an exceptional parameter set of Hausdorff dimension zero and the no-overlap formula when $\lambda>2^{-2/3}$ (Rapaport and Varjú 2024, Corollary 1.4 and Theorem 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[RapaportVarju2024](#ref-RapaportVarju2024)]. Rapaport–Varjú proved the entropy-rate formula, including exact overlaps, for rational translations and a positive common ratio (Rapaport and Varjú 2024, Theorem A.1) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[RapaportVarju2024](#ref-RapaportVarju2024)]. Feng–Feng proved the no-exact-overlap formula for algebraic translations and a signed common ratio (Feng and Feng 2025, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[FengFeng2025](#ref-FengFeng2025)]. The distinction in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is that the entropy rate of the full affine-map walk is sharp even when exact collisions occur, with no arithmetic or separation assumption.

Absence of exact overlaps does not itself supply a quantitative separation estimate. Baker (Baker 2021, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Baker2021](#ref-Baker2021)] and, independently, Bárány–Käenmäki (Bárány and Käenmäki 2021, Theorem 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[BaranyKaenmaki2021](#ref-BaranyKaenmaki2021)] constructed systems without exact overlaps whose distinct cylinders approach one another at arbitrarily prescribed superexponential rates. Thus the entropy carried by extremely close maps must be handled without bounding the smallest positive separation.

Our proof combines the entropy retained by this map walk with a finite-law estimate that detects information across arbitrarily fine scales. It uses translation averaging, developed by Wang (Wang 2011, sec. 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Wang2011](#ref-Wang2011)], and the entropy calculus and fair-pair arguments of Varjú (Varjú 2019a, sec. 2.2, Propositions 20–21, and Lemma 22) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[VarjuAbsolute2019](#ref-VarjuAbsolute2019)]. Uniform nonsaturation has an antecedent for Bernoulli convolutions in Breuillard–Varjú (Breuillard and Varjú 2019, Lemma 13) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[BreuillardVarju2019](#ref-BreuillardVarju2019)]. We prove the required entropy estimates here; the only external dimension theorem used in the proof is exact dimensionality.

The argument has three steps. First, for a finite real law $\nu$, compare its exact entropy with the entropy of its grid cell at a fixed scale. The difference is information hidden inside those cells. A bound for the sum of several independent copies forces this information to be witnessed by fair (equal-weight) two-point laws at smaller scales. The estimate is independent of the smallest distance between support points.

Second, group the coding symbols into blocks and condition on their symbol counts. This is the block-type disintegration of Galicer–Saglietti–Shmerkin–Yavicoli (Galicer et al. 2016, sec. 6.4 and Lemma 6.6) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[GalicerSagliettiShmerkinYavicoli2016](#ref-GalicerSagliettiShmerkinYavicoli2016)], also developed by Saglietti–Shmerkin–Solomyak (Saglietti, Shmerkin, and Solomyak 2018, Lemma 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[SagliettiShmerkinSolomyak2018](#ref-SagliettiShmerkinSolomyak2018)]; Käenmäki–Orponen (Käenmäki and Orponen 2023, sec. 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[KaenmakiOrponen2023](#ref-KaenmakiOrponen2023)] explicitly allow repeated indexed maps in this construction. Here the counts fix each block’s signed contraction. For a sequence of such blocks, its translation then identifies its complete composed map. Conditioning costs only the entropy of the counts, so the map-entropy rate gives a lower bound for the remaining translation entropy even when addresses collide. The same fixed contractions give a small sumset bound. A hypothetical dimension deficit therefore yields a linear amount of fair-pair mass across a finite band of very fine scales.

Third, each pair produces a positive entropy gain against the unobserved tail. The bands may end at arbitrarily large depths. We choose successive block lengths only after the preceding endpoints are known. At each observation scale their retained blocks are disjoint, so their gains telescope to a bounded total. Averaging over observation scales gives a fixed positive contribution from every band, a contradiction as the number of bands grows.

Section <a href="#sec:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:entropy">2</a> proves the entropy calculus, nonsaturation, and two-point gain. Section <a href="#sec:pairs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pairs">3</a> proves the finite-law estimate. Section <a href="#sec:types" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:types">4</a> applies it to conditional map laws, and Section <a href="#sec:windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:windows">5</a> arranges the disjoint gains and completes the proof. Section <a href="#sec:corollaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:corollaries">6</a> derives the no-overlap measure and attractor formulas, followed by the homogeneous and three-map specializations. No quantitative bound on the fine-scale endpoints is needed.

<a id="sec:upper"></a>
#### The entropy-rate upper bound

We prove the upper bound before turning to the contradiction argument. Fix a positive integer $n$. Use the finite set of distinct maps in the support of $G_n$ as a new alphabet, giving each map $g$ its exact probability $q_g=\mathbb P(G_n=g)>0$. Grouping independent coding symbols into $n$-blocks shows that this new system has the same measure $\mu$. Its one-symbol entropy is $H(G_n)$ and its mean contraction depth is $n\chi$, since coincident maps have the same absolute slope.

For $0<\varepsilon<n\chi$, the strong law shows that almost every address in this block system eventually has, at every length $j$, a prefix of probability at least $2^{-j(H(G_n)+\varepsilon)}$ and absolute contraction at most $2^{-j(n\chi-\varepsilon)}$. There are at most $2^{j(H(G_n)+\varepsilon)}$ such prefixes. For each fixed eventual starting length $J$, let $E_J$ be the closed set of addresses satisfying both conditions for every $j\ge J$. Its compact coding image is covered by intervals of diameter at most $C2^{-j(n\chi-\varepsilon)}$, where $C$ bounds the attractor diameter. The total $a$th power of these diameters tends to zero when $a>(H(G_n)+\varepsilon)/(n\chi-\varepsilon)$. The countable union over eventual starting lengths is a full-measure Borel set with this dimension bound. The case of zero attractor diameter is immediate. Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, then taking the infimum over $n$, gives <a id="eq:upper-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:upper-bound}
 d\le\min\{1,h/\chi\}.$$ In particular, the conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> already holds when $h=0$, including a single-map system. To prove equality in all other cases, it suffices to rule out <a id="eq:dimension-loss"></a>$$\label{eq:dimension-loss}
 d<1,\qquad d\chi<h.$$ These inequalities are the contradiction hypothesis in Sections <a href="#sec:types" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:types">4</a> and <a href="#sec:windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:windows">5</a>.

<a id="sec:entropy"></a>
### Entropy between two scales

We first establish the entropy estimates that turn a separated pair of translations into a definite gain. The decisive property of the self-similar measure is uniform nonsaturation: if its dimension is less than one, every one-bit scale interval leaves a positive entropy deficit. The intermediate entropy identities apply to arbitrary compactly supported probability measures on the line.

For a finite-valued variable $Z$, write $H(Z)=-\sum_z\mathbb P(Z=z)\log\mathbb P(Z=z)$, with $0\log0=0$. Conditional entropy is the average entropy of the conditional law, and $I(Z;Y)=H(Z)-H(Z\mid Y)$; conditional mutual information is defined similarly. We use the entropy chain rule and nonnegativity of conditional mutual information. For a bounded real random variable $X$ and $s>0$, define $$H_s^0(X)=H(\lfloor X/s\rfloor),\qquad
 \mathcal H_s(X)=\int_0^1H(\lfloor X/s+u\rfloor)\,du.$$ We use the same notation for the law of $X$. For $s,t>0$, put $$G_{s,t}=\mathcal H_s-\mathcal H_t,
 \qquad \Delta_s=1-G_{s,2s}.$$ As throughout the paper, all logarithms and entropies are in base $2$. Write $h(f)=-\int f\log f$ for differential entropy and $V_s$ for an independent uniform random variable on $[0,s]$.

The smoothing and nested-grid identities are the averaged-entropy calculus of (Varjú 2019a, sec. 2.2, Lemmas 5, 6, and 10) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[VarjuAbsolute2019](#ref-VarjuAbsolute2019)]; we include their proofs.

<a id="stmt-lem-entropy-calculus"></a>
<div id="lem:entropy-calculus" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:entropy-scale-comparison"></a>
<a id="eq:entropy-grid-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Entropy calculus). *For every compactly supported real law, <a id="eq:entropy-smoothing"></a>$$\label{eq:entropy-smoothing}
 \mathcal H_s(X)=h(X+V_s)-\log s,
 \qquad
 \mathcal H_s(aX+b)=\mathcal H_{s/|a|}(X)
 \quad(a\ne0).$$ If $t/s=N$ is a positive integer, then $G_{s,t}$ is concave in the law, <a id="eq:entropy-integer-window"></a>$$\label{eq:entropy-integer-window}
 0\le G_{s,t}\le\log N,
 \qquad G_{s,t}(X+Y)\ge G_{s,t}(X)$$ for independent bounded $X,Y$. For arbitrary $z\ge s>0$, $$\label{eq:entropy-scale-comparison}
 \mathcal H_s-\log(z/s+2)\le\mathcal H_z\le\mathcal H_s+1.$$ Furthermore, $$\label{eq:entropy-grid-comparison}
 |\mathcal H_s(X)-H_s^0(X)|\le1,
 \qquad |H_s^0(X+b)-H_s^0(X)|\le1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-entropy-calculus"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-entropy-calculus</summary>

*Proof.* The density of $X+V_s$ is $f_s(z)=s^{-1}\mathbb P(X\in[z-s,z))$ almost everywhere. It is bounded by $1/s$ and has bounded support, so its differential entropy is finite, even when the law of $X$ has atoms. Set $p_j(u)=\mathbb P((j-u)s\le X<(j+1-u)s)$. At $z=(j+1-u)s$ we have $p_j(u)=sf_s(z)$ almost everywhere. Changing variables in the sum of the integrals $-p_j(u)\log p_j(u)$ therefore gives $$\mathcal H_s(X)=-\int_{\mathbb R}f_s(z)\log(sf_s(z))\,dz.$$ This proves the first identity. Translation rotates the grid shift modulo $1$, and positive scaling changes the cell length. Reflection also preserves averaged entropy: $-X+V_s$ is a translate of $-(X+V_s')$, where $V_s'=s-V_s$ is independent uniform. These observations prove the signed affine identity.

Suppose $t=Ns$. Let $A$ be independent uniform on $[0,t)$ and define $$F=\left\lfloor\frac{X+A}{s}\right\rfloor,
 \qquad C=\left\lfloor\frac{X+A}{t}\right\rfloor.$$ These are jointly translated nested grids, with $C=\lfloor F/N\rfloor$. Averaging their entropies gives <a id="eq:nested-conditional-entropy"></a>$$\label{eq:nested-conditional-entropy}
 G_{s,t}(X)=H(F\mid C,A).$$ Each coarse cell contains $N$ fine cells, proving the two bounds. For a mixture of laws, conditioning additionally on its mixing variable can only reduce the right-hand side; this proves concavity, including for general probability mixtures. A convolution is a mixture of translates, so translation invariance proves the convolution inequality.

Finally, for any two translated grids of lengths $s\le z$, a $z$-cell intersects at most $\lfloor z/s\rfloor+2$ fine cells, and an $s$-cell intersects at most two coarse cells. The chain rule bounds the two conditional entropies by $\log(z/s+2)$ and $1$. Averaging gives <a href="#eq:entropy-scale-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-scale-comparison">[eq:entropy-scale-comparison]</a>. The same argument for two grids of equal length proves <a href="#eq:entropy-grid-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-grid-comparison">[eq:entropy-grid-comparison]</a>. ◻

</details>

The integer-ratio hypothesis is essential to our use of concavity. In the argument below it is applied only with ratios $2$, a chosen integer $K$, or the square of a chosen integer $M$.

<a id="stmt-lem-entropy-dimension"></a>
<div id="lem:entropy-dimension" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Entropy and dimension). *Let $\nu$ be a compactly supported exact-dimensional probability measure on $\mathbb R$ of dimension $d$. Then <a id="eq:entropy-dimension-upper"></a>$$\label{eq:entropy-dimension-upper}
 \limsup_{s\downarrow0}
 \frac{\max\{\mathcal H_s(\nu),H_s^0(\nu)\}}
 {\log(1/s)}\le d.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-entropy-dimension"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-entropy-dimension</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $\eta>0$. Exact dimensionality gives a measurable set $E$ of mass at least $1-\eta$ and a common threshold $r_0>0$ such that $$\nu(B(x,r))\ge r^{d+\eta}
 \qquad(x\in E, 0<r<r_0).$$ Indeed, the sets where this eventual inequality holds below a fixed reciprocal-integer threshold exhaust a set of full measure; rational radii and monotonicity suffice to make this construction measurable.

For any translated grid of length $s<2r_0$, choose one point of $E$ in each cell meeting $E$. Within each of the three residue classes of cell indices modulo $3$, the chosen points have disjoint balls of radius $s/2$. Each ball has mass at least $(s/2)^{d+\eta}$. Thus at most $3(2/s)^{d+\eta}$ cells meet $E$. Compact support bounds the total number of occupied cells by $C/s$, uniformly over grid translations and small $s$. Conditioning the cell label on membership in $E$ yields $$H(\text{cell label})
 \le1+\nu(E)\log\bigl(3(2/s)^{d+\eta}\bigr)
       +\nu(E^c)\log(C/s)
 \le(d+2\eta)\log(1/s)+O_{\eta,\nu}(1).$$ This controls both the fixed grid and its average. Divide by $\log(1/s)$ and let $\eta\downarrow0$. ◻

</details>

For Bernoulli convolutions, the following nonsaturation property appears in Breuillard–Varjú (Breuillard and Varjú 2019, Lemma 13) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BreuillardVarju2019](#ref-BreuillardVarju2019)]. The stopping-time argument below extends it to unequal contractions of either sign.

<a id="stmt-lem-nonsaturation"></a>
<div id="lem:nonsaturation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Uniform nonsaturation). *Let $\mu$ be a self-similar probability measure for a finite affine system $x\mapsto r_i x+t_i$ with $0<|r_i|<1$. If $\mu$ is exact dimensional with dimension $d<1$, then <a id="eq:uniform-nonsaturation"></a>$$\label{eq:uniform-nonsaturation}
 \delta:=\inf_{v>0}\Delta_v(\mu)>0.$$ No separation or orientation assumption is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-nonsaturation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-nonsaturation</summary>

<a id="eq:one-bit-information"></a>
*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-calculus">2</a>, $0\le\Delta_v\le1$. We show that a vanishing one-bit deficit would force almost maximal entropy in a fixed wider scale interval. Stopped self-similarity then transfers that estimate to every smaller scale, contradicting the entropy bound supplied by exact dimensionality. Suppose there are $v_n>0$ with $\Delta_{v_n}(\mu)\to0$. For $X\sim\mu$, let $\lambda_v$ be the law of $X+V_v$. An independent fair bit $J$ gives $$\label{eq:one-bit-information}
 \Delta_v(\mu)
 =h(X+V_{2v})-h(X+V_v)
 =I(J;X+V_v+vJ).$$ Thus the equal mixture of $\lambda_v$ and its translate by $v$ carries little information about its input label.

Here is a quantitative form of that observation. For two laws $P,Q$, write $\|P-Q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\sup_B|P(B)-Q(B)|$. Observing whether the mixture sample lies in $B$ bounds the information in <a href="#eq:one-bit-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:one-bit-information">[eq:one-bit-information]</a> below by $$h_2\bigl((P(B)+Q(B))/2\bigr)
 -\tfrac12h_2(P(B))-\tfrac12h_2(Q(B)),$$ where $h_2$ is binary entropy. Since $h_2''(u)=-1/(\ln(2)u(1-u))\le-4/\ln2$, this expression is at least $(P(B)-Q(B))^2/(2\ln2)$. Consequently, for $e_v=\|\lambda_v-T_v\lambda_v\|_{\mathrm{TV}}$, where $T_v$ denotes translation by $v$, <a id="eq:small-information-tv"></a>$$\label{eq:small-information-tv}
 e_v\le\sqrt{2\ln(2)\Delta_v(\mu)}.$$

<a id="eq:large-window-saturation"></a>
Fix an integer $K\ge2$. The laws $P_i=T_{iv}\lambda_v$, $0\le i<K$, have densities $f_i$ and satisfy $\|P_i-P_0\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\le i e_v$. Their common density $f_*:=\min_{0\le i<K}f_i$ has mass $m_v$ with <a id="eq:common-density-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:common-density-mass}
 1-m_v\le\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}\int(f_0-f_i)_+
 \le\frac{K(K-1)}2 e_v.$$ For each input label, decompose its law into this same common submeasure and a residual submeasure of mass $1-m_v$. The common/residual flag can be sampled independently of the input label. On the common branch the output gives no information; on the residual branch it gives at most $\log K$. The mutual information of the uniform mixture is therefore at most $(1-m_v)\log K$. That mixture is $\mu*\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(V_{Kv})$, and all its conditional differential entropies equal $h(X+V_v)$. For this fixed $K$, $$\label{eq:large-window-saturation}
 G_{v_n,Kv_n}(\mu)\longrightarrow\log K.$$

We next transfer this near-saturation to every smaller scale. Put $a_*:=\min_i|r_i|>0$. For $0<s<v$, stop the independent coding at the first prefix whose absolute contraction is at most $s/v$. The stopping time is bounded because $\max_i|r_i|<1$. The stopped contractions have absolute values in $(a_*s/v,s/v]$, and the remaining coding is independent with law $\mu$. Thus $\mu$ is a finite mixture of affine images of itself with these contractions. Signed scaling and integer-ratio concavity give <a id="eq:stopped-scale-transfer"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 G_{s,Ks}(\mu)
 &\ge\inf_{z\in[v,v/a_*]}G_{z,Kz}(\mu)\notag\\
 &\ge G_{v,Kv}(\mu)-C_* ,
 \qquad C_*:=\log(1/a_*+2)+1.
 \label{eq:stopped-scale-transfer}
\end{aligned}$$ For the second inequality, apply <a href="#eq:entropy-scale-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-scale-comparison">[eq:entropy-scale-comparison]</a> at $(v,z)$ and $(Kv,Kz)$. The loss $C_*$ is independent of $K$ and the scales.

Choose an integer $K$ with $(1-d)\log K>C_*+2$. Then choose one $v=v_n$ such that $G_{v,Kv}(\mu)>\log K-1$, using <a href="#eq:large-window-saturation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-window-saturation">[eq:large-window-saturation]</a>. For every $0<s<v$, $$G_{s,Ks}(\mu)>B:=\log K-C_*-1>d\log K.$$ At $s_m=vK^{-m}$, telescoping gives $$\mathcal H_{s_m}(\mu)-\mathcal H_v(\mu)
 =\sum_{j=1}^mG_{vK^{-j},vK^{-(j-1)}}(\mu)>mB.$$ Its entropy ratio has lower limit at least $B/\log K>d$, contradicting Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-dimension">3</a>. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

The next estimate explains how the deficit produces gain. Unlike the concavity statements, it does not require an integer ratio of scales. The fair-pair comparison follows the method of (Varjú 2019a, Proposition 20 and Lemma 22) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[VarjuAbsolute2019](#ref-VarjuAbsolute2019)].

<a id="stmt-lem-two-point"></a>
<div id="lem:two-point" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:two-point-gain"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Two-point gain). *Let $X$ be bounded and $J$ an independent fair bit. For $0<s<v<t$, $$\label{eq:two-point-gain}
 G_{s,t}(X+vJ)-G_{s,t}(X)
 \ge\Delta_v(X)-s/v-v/t.$$ The same estimate holds for convolution with any fair two-point law whose two points have distance $v$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-two-point"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-two-point</summary>

*Proof.* Put $Y=X+vJ$. For each $a>0$, let $U_a$ be independent uniform on $[0,1)$ and write $Z_a=\lfloor Y/a+U_a\rfloor$. Translation invariance of the averaged entropy gives $$D_a:=\mathcal H_a(Y)-\mathcal H_a(X)=I(J;Z_a\mid U_a).$$ Uniform smoothing also gives $D_v=h(X+V_{2v})-h(X+V_v)=\Delta_v(X)$.

To compare $s$ and $v$, use independent shifts $U_s,U_v$ and the same sample $Y$. The information chain rule gives $$I(J;Z_v\mid U_s,U_v)
 \le I(J;Z_s\mid U_s,U_v)
       +H(Z_v\mid Z_s,U_s,U_v).$$ The first two terms are $D_v$ and $D_s$ because the unused shift is independent. For each fixed fine grid and each of its cells of positive probability, the conditional sample law is independent of $U_v$. That cell, of length $s<v$, contains a boundary of the $v$-grid on a proportion $s/v$ of its shifts. Otherwise the coarse label is fixed; when a boundary occurs there are at most two coarse labels. Averaging therefore gives $H(Z_v\mid Z_s,U_s,U_v)\le s/v$, whence $D_s\ge D_v-s/v$.

For the coarse-scale gain, adjoining $X$ to the observation yields $$D_t\le I(J;Z_t,X\mid U_t)=I(J;Z_t\mid X,U_t).$$ For fixed $X=x$, the two labels at $x$ and $x+v$ differ on a proportion $v/t$ of shifts. Their conditional information is then one bit and is otherwise zero. Thus $D_t\le v/t$. Subtracting proves <a href="#eq:two-point-gain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-point-gain">[eq:two-point-gain]</a>. The proof allows atoms, since boundary coincidences occur on null sets of shifts. Finally, any fair pair is a translate of $\{0,v\}$ after ordering its two points; translation invariance proves the last assertion, including for reflected pairs. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:pairs"></a>
### Pair mass below a fixed scale

We next consider a finite probability law without any separation assumption. Its exact entropy may greatly exceed its entropy at a prescribed scale. The following lemma detects that excess through the mass that can be placed in fair pairs at smaller scales. An upper bound for a sumset will make the lemma useful for the block laws in the next section. Varjú’s scale-local decomposition into fair two-point submeasures (Varjú 2019a, Proposition 21) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[VarjuAbsolute2019](#ref-VarjuAbsolute2019)] is a predecessor of this approach. Here the sumset term controls the total capacity over all finer bands; we prove the needed estimate by randomized nested partitions.

<a id="stmt-def-pair-capacity"></a>
<div id="def:pair-capacity" class="definition">

**Definition 6** (Pair capacity). Let $\nu$ be a probability law with finite support $F\subset\mathbb R$. For $\ell\in\mathbb Z$, let $\mathcal E_\ell(F)$ be the set of unordered pairs $\{x,y\}\subset F$ satisfying $2^{-\ell}\le |x-y|<2^{1-\ell}$. Define $c_\ell(\nu)$ as the maximum of $$\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_\ell(F)}w_e
 \quad\text{subject to}\quad
 w_e\ge0,\qquad
 \frac12\sum_{e\ni x}w_e\le\nu(\{x\})\quad(x\in F).$$ When $\mathcal E_\ell(F)$ is empty, this maximum is zero.

</div>

Thus $c_\ell(\nu)$ is the largest total weight of fair two-point laws in the indicated distance band whose weighted sum is a submeasure of $\nu$. Summing the constraints gives $\sum_e w_e\le1$; the feasible set is compact, so the maximum exists and $0\le c_\ell(\nu)\le1$. For example, if $\nu=p\delta_x+(1-p)\delta_y$ with $x\ne y$, then the capacity in the band containing $|x-y|$ is $2\min\{p,1-p\}$, and all its other capacities vanish.

<a id="stmt-lem-pair-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:pair-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:pair-bound"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Finite-law pair bound). *For every integer $k\ge1$ there is a finite constant $C_k$ such that every finitely supported probability law $\nu$ on $\mathbb R$ and every $\rho>0$ satisfy $$\label{eq:pair-bound}
 k\bigl(H(\nu)-H^0_\rho(\nu)\bigr)
 \le \log|kF|
   +C_k\sum_{\ell:\,2^{-\ell}\le2\rho}c_\ell(\nu),
 \qquad F=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu,$$ where $kF=\{x_1+\cdots+x_k:x_i\in F\}$. The constant is independent of the smallest positive distance in $F$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pair-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pair-bound</summary>

*Proof.* Let $X_1,\ldots,X_k$ be independent with law $\nu$, write $\mathbf X=(X_1,\ldots,X_k)$, and put $Y=X_1+\cdots+X_k$. Knowing the initial length-$\rho$ cells leaves $k(H(\nu)-H^0_\rho(\nu))$ bits of uncertainty about $\mathbf X$. Revealing $Y$ costs at most $\log|kF|$ bits. We bound the remaining uncertainty by successively refining those cells. The refinement cuts are random, independent of the samples, to prevent atoms close to a fixed boundary from being charged too often.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### The partitions.

<a id="eq:pair-cut-density"></a>
Set $\rho_h=\rho4^{-h}$ for integers $h\ge0$, and start with the half-open grid intervals of length $\rho$. Suppose a parent at level $h$ has length $L\in[\rho_h/2,2\rho_h]$. Let $N$ be a nearest integer to $L/\rho_{h+1}$, with ties resolved by a fixed rule. Then $2\le N\le8$. Divide the parent into $N$ equal pieces, and perturb its internal cuts independently and uniformly by amounts in $[-\rho_{h+1}/10,\rho_{h+1}/10]$. The unperturbed child lengths belong to $[0.75\rho_{h+1},1.25\rho_{h+1}]$; after perturbation they belong to $[0.55\rho_{h+1},1.45\rho_{h+1}]$. Thus the cuts remain ordered and every child has length in $[\rho_{h+1}/2,2\rho_{h+1}]$. Conditional on the previous partitions, every new cut has density at most $$\label{eq:pair-cut-density}
 \frac{5}{\rho_{h+1}}=\frac{20}{\rho_h}.$$ All cuts are sampled independently of $\mathbf X$; a parent that contains several coordinates uses the same cuts for each coordinate.

If $F$ is a singleton, the lemma is immediate. Otherwise choose an integer $J$ such that $$2\rho_J<\min\{|x-y|:x,y\in F,\ x\ne y\}.$$ Every level-$J$ cell then contains at most one point of $F$, for every realization of the partitions. No bound on $J$ will be needed.

<a id="eq:pair-chain"></a>
For a fixed partition scheme, denote by $\mathbf C_h$ the tuple of level-$h$ cells containing the samples. Nesting and separation at level $J$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 k\bigl(H(\nu)-H^0_\rho(\nu)\bigr)
 &=H(\mathbf X\mid\mathbf C_0)\notag\\
 &\le\log|kF|+H(\mathbf X\mid\mathbf C_0,Y)\notag\\
 &=\log|kF|+
   \sum_{h=0}^{J-1}H(\mathbf C_{h+1}\mid\mathbf C_h,Y).
 \label{eq:pair-chain}
\end{aligned}$$ The whole partition scheme is sampled independently of $\mathbf X$. We now average this finite chain over the schemes; equivalently, the conditional entropies on its right also condition on that scheme. For the summand at level $h$, the cell labels involved use only cuts through level $h+1$. Conditional on those cuts, all later cuts are independent of $\mathbf X$ and hence of $Y$ and these labels, so they may be discarded from the conditioning.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### One refinement after observing the sum.

<a id="eq:pair-two-exceptions"></a>
Fix the partitions through level $h$, and condition first on a tuple of current cells $A_1,\ldots,A_k$ of positive probability. The coordinates remain independent, with laws $\nu(\cdot\mid A_i)$. Write $m_i=\mathbb EX_i$ in this conditional law. Each mean belongs to its half-open parent, since it is an average of finitely many points of that parent. Fix the new cuts and let $$a=\min_{\substack{1\le i\le k\\
              c\text{ a new internal cut of }A_i}}|m_i-c|.$$ This minimum is positive almost surely in the new cuts. Call coordinate $i$ exceptional when $|X_i-m_i|\ge a/(4k)$, let $p_i$ be its probability, and put $t=\sum_i p_i$. Let $E$ be the event that at least two coordinates are exceptional, and write $q=\mathbb P(E)$. Independence, before conditioning on $Y$, gives $$\label{eq:pair-two-exceptions}
 q\le\sum_{i<j}p_ip_j\le t^2,
 \qquad q\le t.$$ Also put $$B=\left\{\left|Y-\sum_i m_i\right|\ge a/2\right\}.$$ This second event is known once $Y$ is known, and $\mathbb P(B)\le t$: if every coordinate is nonexceptional, the sum deviation is less than $a/4$.

<a id="eq:pair-flag"></a>
On $E^c\cap B^c$, every child label equals the child label of its mean. Indeed, a changed label forces $|X_i-m_i|\ge a$ for some $i$, including a change at a half-open endpoint. There is then at most one exceptional coordinate, so $$\left|\sum_j(X_j-m_j)\right|
 \ge a-\frac{k-1}{4k}a>a/2,$$ a contradiction. Each coordinate has at most eight child labels. In the next display, conditioning on the fixed partitions and new cuts is implicit. By revealing the flag $1_E$ in addition to $Y$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 H(\mathbf C_{h+1}\mid Y,\mathbf C_h=(A_i))
 &\le h_2(q)+3k\bigl(q+\mathbb P(B)\bigr)\notag\\
 &\le (3+6k)t\le9k\sum_i p_i.
 \label{eq:pair-flag}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $h_2(q)\le3\sqrt q$ and <a href="#eq:pair-two-exceptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-two-exceptions">[eq:pair-two-exceptions]</a> were used. The elementary binary entropy bound follows from $-(1-q)\ln(1-q)\le q$ and $-\sqrt q\ln q\le2/e$. There is no binary-entropy cost for $B$, since it is determined by the conditioned observation $Y$. This distinction is what makes <a href="#eq:pair-flag" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-flag">[eq:pair-flag]</a> linear in the exceptional probabilities.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### Averaging the cuts and converting to pairs.

For a fixed value of $X_i$, set $u=|X_i-m_i|$. The event $a\le4ku$ requires one of at most $7k$ cuts to be within $4ku$ of its corresponding mean. The density bound <a href="#eq:pair-cut-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-cut-density">[eq:pair-cut-density]</a> and a union bound give $$\mathbb P_{\rm cuts}(a\le4ku)
 \le7k\frac{20}{\rho_h}(8ku)
 =1120k^2\frac{u}{\rho_h}.$$ Consequently $$\mathbb E_{\rm cuts}p_i
 \le\frac{1120k^2}{\rho_h}\mathbb E|X_i-m_i|
 \le\frac{1120k^2}{\rho_h}\mathbb E|X_i-X_i'|,$$ where $X_i'$ is an independent copy in the same current cell. The last inequality is Jensen’s inequality. These expectations still use the current-cell conditional laws.

<a id="eq:pair-distance-cost"></a>
For a fixed level-$h$ partition, integrating the cells out gives the symmetric coupling $$\pi_h=\sum_{A:\,\nu(A)>0}\nu(A)
 \bigl(\nu(\cdot\mid A)\otimes\nu(\cdot\mid A)\bigr).$$ Both its marginals equal $\nu$, and its pairs have distance at most $2\rho_h$. Each of the $k$ coordinate contributions becomes the same integral against $\pi_h$. Thus <a href="#eq:pair-flag" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-flag">[eq:pair-flag]</a>, averaged over the new cuts and current cells, is at most $$\label{eq:pair-distance-cost}
 \frac{10080k^4}{\rho_h}\int|x-y|\,d\pi_h(x,y).$$

<a id="eq:pair-one-level"></a>
For each band $2^{-\ell}\le|x-y|<2^{1-\ell}$, the corresponding portion of $\pi_h$ supplies a feasible mixture in Definition <a href="#def:pair-capacity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:pair-capacity">6</a>. Explicitly, the weight assigned to the unordered pair $\{x,y\}$ is $\pi_h(x,y)+\pi_h(y,x)=2\pi_h(x,y)$. The resulting submeasure has mass $\sum_{y:\,2^{-\ell}\le|x-y|<2^{1-\ell}}\pi_h(x,y)$ at $x$, which is at most $\nu(\{x\})$. Its total weight equals the mass of that band under $\pi_h$. Therefore <a href="#eq:pair-distance-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-distance-cost">[eq:pair-distance-cost]</a> is at most $$\label{eq:pair-one-level}
 20160k^4
 \sum_{\ell:\,2^{-\ell}\le2\rho_h}
 \frac{2^{-\ell}}{\rho_h}c_\ell(\nu).$$ This estimate also holds after averaging the previous partitions; its right side refers only to the original law $\nu$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### Summing all finer scales.

For a fixed $\ell$, the factors $2^{-\ell}/\rho_h$ grow by four at each step and are summed only while they are at most two. Hence $$\sum_{h\ge0:\,2^{-\ell}\le2\rho_h}
       \frac{2^{-\ell}}{\rho_h}\le\frac83.$$ Summing <a href="#eq:pair-one-level" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-one-level">[eq:pair-one-level]</a> in <a href="#eq:pair-chain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-chain">[eq:pair-chain]</a> proves <a href="#eq:pair-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-bound">[eq:pair-bound]</a>; one may take $C_k=60000k^4$. The separating depth $J$ disappears from the bound. In particular, the estimate includes separations smaller than any prescribed exponential scale. ◻

</details>

For a fixed finite law only finitely many pair capacities are nonzero. Later we will apply Lemma <a href="#lem:pair-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pair-bound">7</a> to a finite family of finite laws for each fixed block count. Their nonzero capacities therefore lie in a common finite range, although the upper endpoint need not have any effective bound.

<a id="sec:types"></a>
### Conditioning on block types

<a id="eq:types-loss"></a>
Let $\Phi=(\varphi_i)_{i\in\Lambda}$ be a finite affine iterated function system on $\mathbb R$, where $\varphi_i(x)=r_ix+t_i$ and $0<|r_i|<1$. Let $p=(p_i)_{i\in\Lambda}$ be a strictly positive probability vector, and let $\mu$ be its self-similar measure. Write $G_n$ for the random complete affine map obtained from $n$ independent symbols with law $p$, and let $h=\lim_{n\to\infty}H(G_n)/n$ be its entropy rate. Throughout this section assume $$\label{eq:types-loss}
 d:=\dim_H\mu<1,
 \qquad d\chi<h,
 \qquad \chi=-\sum_{i\in\Lambda}p_i\log|r_i|.$$ All logarithms have base $2$. We will find finite conditional coding laws whose exact entropy exceeds their entropy at a prescribed scale by a positive multiple of the number of blocks. Lemma <a href="#lem:pair-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pair-bound">7</a> will then turn this deficit into pair mass across finite bands of arbitrarily fine scales.

We condition on the number of occurrences of each symbol in a short block. These counts fix its signed contraction. Once all block contractions are fixed, the translation determines the complete composed map. The entropy cost of recording the counts is small, so the conditional translations retain almost all the map entropy. This argument allows distinct words to define the same map. The block-type disintegration is due to Galicer–Saglietti–Shmerkin–Yavicoli (Galicer et al. 2016, sec. 6.4, Lemma 6.6) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[GalicerSagliettiShmerkinYavicoli2016](#ref-GalicerSagliettiShmerkinYavicoli2016)] and is developed further by Saglietti–Shmerkin–Solomyak (Saglietti, Shmerkin, and Solomyak 2018, Lemma 6.2) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[SagliettiShmerkinSolomyak2018](#ref-SagliettiShmerkinSolomyak2018)]; Käenmäki–Orponen explicitly allow repeated indexed maps (Käenmäki and Orponen 2023, sec. 2.2, Proposition 2.8) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[KaenmakiOrponen2023](#ref-KaenmakiOrponen2023)]. We retain the signed contraction and estimate the entropy of the complete map after collisions have been combined.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Parameters and conditional coding laws

<a id="eq:types-parameters"></a>
Write $m=|\Lambda|$. The second inequality in <a href="#eq:types-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-loss">[eq:types-loss]</a> implies $h>0$, hence $m\ge2$. Choose $\alpha>\chi$ such that $d\alpha<h$. For $d>0$ we may take $\chi<\alpha<h/d$; for $d=0$ we may take $\alpha=\chi+1$. Choose an integer $b\ge1$ sufficiently large that $$m\log(b+1)<b\bigl(h-d\alpha\bigr),$$ and set $$\label{eq:types-parameters}
 A=b\alpha,
 \qquad A'=\frac{b(\chi+\alpha)}2,
 \qquad h_b=bh-m\log(b+1).$$ Then <a id="eq:types-gap"></a>$$\label{eq:types-gap}
 b\chi<A'<A,
 \qquad \gamma:=h_b-dA>0.$$ These parameters remain fixed throughout the rest of the proof.

Group the iid symbols with law $p$ into words $W_0,W_1,\ldots\in\Lambda^b$. For $w\in\Lambda^b$ let $\operatorname{type}(w)=(\kappa_i)_{i\in\Lambda}$, where $\kappa_i$ counts the occurrences of $i$ in $w$. The set $\mathcal T_b$ of types consists of the nonnegative integer vectors with $\sum_i\kappa_i=b$, and has cardinality at most $(b+1)^m$. For $\kappa\in\mathcal T_b$, define $$N_\kappa=\frac{b!}{\prod_i\kappa_i!},
 \qquad q_\kappa=N_\kappa\prod_i p_i^{\kappa_i},
 \qquad Q_\kappa
   =\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}\bigl(W_0\mid\operatorname{type}(W_0)=\kappa\bigr).$$ Every word of type $\kappa$ has probability $\prod_i p_i^{\kappa_i}$, so $Q_\kappa$ is uniform on its $N_\kappa$ words. Equivalently, we can generate the original coding by first choosing independent types $\omega_0,\omega_1,\ldots$ with distribution $q$, then choosing the words independently with respective conditional laws $Q_{\omega_0},Q_{\omega_1},\ldots$. This follows by multiplying the probabilities of any finite sequence of blocks. In particular it preserves the original, possibly unequal, symbol weights $p_i$.

Write $\omega=(\omega_h)_{h\ge0}$ for the type sequence and $\theta\omega=(\omega_{h+1})_{h\ge0}$ for its shift. For $j\ge0$, let $\mathcal F_j$ be the sigma-algebra generated by $\omega_0,\ldots,\omega_{j-1}$; $\mathcal F_0$ is trivial. For a type $\kappa$ and an environment $\omega$, define <a id="eq:types-products"></a>$$\label{eq:types-products}
 r(\kappa)=\prod_{i\in\Lambda}r_i^{\kappa_i},
 \qquad R_j=R_j(\omega)=\prod_{h<j}r(\omega_h),
 \qquad \sigma_j=-\log|R_j|,$$ with $R_0=1$ and $\sigma_0=0$. Thus $R_j$ is $\mathcal F_j$-measurable, including its sign. Let $$D=\max_{\kappa\in\mathcal T_b}\bigl(-\log|r(\kappa)|\bigr).$$ The increments of $\sigma_j$ are iid bounded positive random variables of mean $b\chi$, and $0\le\sigma_j\le Dj$.

<a id="eq:types-laws"></a>
Given $\omega$, use the independent conditional block draws above to define $$\label{eq:types-laws}
 \nu_{\omega,n}
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}_{\omega}\!\left(\sum_{h=0}^{n-1}
       R_h\varphi_{W_h}(0)\right),
 \qquad
 \mu_\omega
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}_{\omega}\!\left(\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}
       R_h\varphi_{W_h}(0)\right).$$ Here $\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}_\omega$ denotes the law under those conditional word draws. The infinite sum converges uniformly, since the block translations form a finite set and $\max_i|r_i|<1$. The law $\nu_{\omega,n}$ depends only on $\mathcal F_n$, whereas $\mu_\omega$ can depend on the entire environment. Averaging over the types gives $\mu=\mathbb E\mu_\omega$. For any real $u\ne0$ and any law $\lambda$, write $\mathsf D_u\lambda=\mathop{\mathrm{Law}}(uX)$ when $X$ has law $\lambda$; this notation retains the sign of $u$.

For $n\ge1$, let $\Omega_n=(\omega_0,\ldots,\omega_{n-1})$ be the tuple of the first $n$ types, and put <a id="eq:types-scales"></a>$$\label{eq:types-scales}
 \rho_n=2^{-\lceil An\rceil},
 \qquad E_n=\{\omega:\sigma_n\le A'n\},
 \qquad a_n=\lceil An\rceil-1.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-type-deficit"></a>
<div id="lem:type-deficit" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:types-exact-entropy"></a>
<a id="eq:types-grid-entropy"></a>
<a id="eq:types-deficit"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Entropy retained by types). *For a system satisfying <a href="#eq:types-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-loss">[eq:types-loss]</a>, with the parameters <a href="#eq:types-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-parameters">[eq:types-parameters]</a> and conditional laws <a href="#eq:types-laws" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-laws">[eq:types-laws]</a>, for every $n\ge1$ and $s>0$ we have $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb EH(\nu_{\omega,n})
 &=H(G_{bn}\mid\Omega_n)
 \ge nh_b,\label{eq:types-exact-entropy}\\
 \mathbb EH_s^0(\nu_{\omega,n})
 &\le H_s^0(\mu)+1.\label{eq:types-grid-entropy}
\end{aligned}$$ Moreover, there are $\varepsilon>0$ and an integer $n_0$ such that for every $n\ge n_0$, $$\label{eq:types-deficit}
 \mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf1_{E_n}
    \bigl(H(\nu_{\omega,n})-H_{\rho_n}^0(\nu_{\omega,n})\bigr)
    \right]\ge\varepsilon n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-type-deficit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-type-deficit</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $\Omega_n$. The composition corresponding to a tuple $(w_0,\ldots,w_{n-1})$ of allowed blocks is $$\varphi_{w_0\cdots w_{n-1}}(x)
   =R_nx+\sum_{h<n}R_h\varphi_{w_h}(0).$$ Its signed slope $R_n$ is fixed by $\Omega_n$, and its translation has law $\nu_{\omega,n}$. Thus the complete map and its translation determine one another under this conditioning, even when several words give the same map. It follows that $$\mathbb EH(\nu_{\omega,n})=H(G_{bn}\mid\Omega_n).$$ There are at most $(b+1)^m$ types, and the block types are independent, so $H(\Omega_n)\le nm\log(b+1)$. The entropy-rate definition gives $H(G_{bn})\ge bnh$. Therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 H(G_{bn}\mid\Omega_n)
 &\ge H(G_{bn})-H(\Omega_n)\\
 &\ge bnh-nm\log(b+1)=nh_b.
\end{aligned}$$ This conditioning inequality does not require $\Omega_n$ to be a function of $G_{bn}$; equal maps may arise from different type tuples. This proves <a href="#eq:types-exact-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-exact-entropy">[eq:types-exact-entropy]</a>.

<a id="eq:types-grid-translation"></a>
We next compare the finite sum with the original measure at a fixed grid scale. Translation changes fixed-grid entropy by at most one bit: for any bounded random variable $X$ and $t\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{eq:types-grid-translation}
 H_s^0(X+t)\ge H_s^0(X)-1.$$ This is the fixed-grid bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-calculus">2</a>, Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-grid-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-grid-comparison">[eq:entropy-grid-comparison]</a>.

Condition now only on $\mathcal F_n$. The original symbols after the first $bn$ positions are still independent with law $p$, and are independent of the prefix. Consequently the full coding law conditional on $\mathcal F_n$ is $$\lambda_\omega
   =\nu_{\omega,n}*\mathsf D_{R_n}\mu,
 \qquad \mu=\mathbb E\lambda_\omega.$$ For each fixed type prefix this convolution is a mixture of translations of $\nu_{\omega,n}$. Applying concavity of fixed-grid entropy first to these translations and then to the type mixture, and using <a href="#eq:types-grid-translation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-grid-translation">[eq:types-grid-translation]</a>, gives $$H_s^0(\mu)
 \ge\mathbb EH_s^0(\lambda_\omega)
 \ge\mathbb EH_s^0(\nu_{\omega,n})-1.$$ All entropies are finite because the laws have bounded support. The sign of $R_n$ affects only the random translation in this argument. This proves <a href="#eq:types-grid-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-grid-entropy">[eq:types-grid-entropy]</a>.

<a id="eq:types-deficit-bound"></a>
Set $V_n=H(\nu_{\omega,n})-H_{\rho_n}^0(\nu_{\omega,n})$. Quantization is a function of the exact finite value, so $$\label{eq:types-deficit-bound}
 0\le V_n\le H(\nu_{\omega,n})\le nb\log m.$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-dimension">3</a>, $\limsup_{s\downarrow0}H_s^0(\mu)/\log(1/s)\le d$. Choose $\eta=\gamma/(4A)>0$. For all sufficiently large $n$, the preceding estimates give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb EV_n
 &\ge nh_b-(d+\eta)\lceil An\rceil-1\\
 &\ge\tfrac34\gamma n-(d+\eta)-1
 \ge\tfrac12\gamma n.
\end{aligned}$$ The law of large numbers and $b\chi<A'$ imply $\mathbb P(E_n^c)\to0$. By <a href="#eq:types-deficit-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-deficit-bound">[eq:types-deficit-bound]</a>, after increasing the threshold on $n$, $$\mathbb E[\mathbf1_{E_n^c}V_n]
 \le nb\log m\,\mathbb P(E_n^c)\le\tfrac14\gamma n.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:types-deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-deficit">[eq:types-deficit]</a> holds with $\varepsilon=\gamma/4$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### From the deficit to bands of pair mass

The preceding lemma produces entropy that is invisible at scale $\rho_n$. To apply the finite-law estimate, we must also control the number of possible sums of several independent copies of a conditional prefix. Fixing the types makes that count uniform in the environment.

<a id="stmt-prop-pair-bands"></a>
<div id="prop:pair-bands" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:types-pair-bands"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Pair mass in finite scale bands). *Under the assumptions and notation of Lemma <a href="#lem:type-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:type-deficit">8</a>, there exist $c>0$ and an integer $n_0$ such that, for each $n\ge n_0$, there is a deterministic finite integer $B_n\ge a_n$ satisfying $$\label{eq:types-pair-bands}
 \mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf1_{E_n}
       \sum_{\ell=a_n}^{B_n}c_\ell(\nu_{\omega,n})\right]\ge cn.$$ Here $c_\ell(\nu)$ is the maximum total weight of fair two-point laws with separations in $[2^{-\ell},2^{1-\ell})$ whose weighted sum is a submeasure of $\nu$, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:pair-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pair-bound">7</a>. The constants $b,A,A',c,n_0$ are independent of $n$ and $\omega$; no growth bound on $B_n$ is asserted.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-pair-bands"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-pair-bands</summary>

<a id="eq:types-sumset"></a>
*Proof.* Let $\mathcal D_b=\{\varphi_w(0):w\in\Lambda^b\}$. It has at most $m^b$ elements. For fixed types, write $S=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\nu_{\omega,n}$. Since every $R_h$ is fixed by the types, $$S\subseteq\sum_{h<n}R_h\mathcal D_b,
 \qquad kS\subseteq\sum_{h<n}R_h(k\mathcal D_b)
 \quad(k\ge1).$$ A sum of $k$ elements of $\mathcal D_b$ is specified by the number of occurrences of each digit. Each count belongs to $\{0,\ldots,k\}$, so $|k\mathcal D_b|\le(k+1)^{m^b}$ and hence $$\label{eq:types-sumset}
 \log|kS|\le nm^b\log(k+1).$$ This is an upper bound regardless of any collisions among the represented sums and regardless of the signs of the $R_h$.

With $b$ and $\varepsilon$ already fixed, choose an integer $k$ so large that $$m^b\log(k+1)<\frac{k\varepsilon}{2}.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:pair-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pair-bound">7</a> to each $\nu_{\omega,n}$ at scale $\rho_n$. Its conclusion, with a positive constant $C_k$ depending only on $k$, is $$k\bigl(H(\nu_{\omega,n})-H_{\rho_n}^0(\nu_{\omega,n})\bigr)
 \le\log|kS|+C_k\sum_{\ell\ge a_n}c_\ell(\nu_{\omega,n});$$ the index condition follows from $2^{-\ell}\le2\rho_n$ if and only if $\ell\ge a_n$. Multiply by $\mathbf1_{E_n}$ and take expectations. Lemma <a href="#lem:type-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:type-deficit">8</a> and <a href="#eq:types-sumset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-sumset">[eq:types-sumset]</a> give $$C_k\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf1_{E_n}
       \sum_{\ell\ge a_n}c_\ell(\nu_{\omega,n})\right]
 \ge k\varepsilon n-nm^b\log(k+1)
 \ge\frac{k\varepsilon n}{2}.$$

For fixed $n$ there are finitely many type prefixes and thus finitely many laws $\nu_{\omega,n}$, all with finite support. Collect the dyadic band indices of all distinct pairs of support points in all these laws into a finite set $J_n\subset\mathbb Z$, and set $$B_n=\max\bigl(\{a_n\}\cup J_n\bigr).$$ For every environment, $c_\ell(\nu_{\omega,n})=0$ when $\ell>B_n$. Thus the infinite sum in the last inequality equals the sum from $a_n$ to $B_n$, and <a href="#eq:types-pair-bands" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:types-pair-bands">[eq:types-pair-bands]</a> follows with $c=k\varepsilon/(2C_k)$. ◻

</details>

The bands in Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-bands" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-bands">9</a> can extend far beyond the scale $2^{-An}$. Their finiteness is enough: the next step will select successive block lengths after the earlier upper endpoints $B_n$ are known. No quantitative separation of distinct cylinder translations has been used.

<a id="sec:windows"></a>
### Disjoint windows of entropy gain

Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-bands" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-bands">9</a> supplies a linear amount of fair-pair weight in a finite interval of logarithmic depths for every sufficiently long block segment. We now turn this weight into entropy gain. The difficulty is that the depth interval may extend arbitrarily far. We choose several segments whose depth intervals are separated, and show that their gains occupy disjoint windows of block indices. At each physical scale the total gain is bounded, whereas every chosen interval contributes the same positive amount after averaging over scales.

Throughout this section, retain the environment and constants from Section <a href="#sec:types" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:types">4</a>. Thus $\sigma_j=-\log|R_j|$ is the contraction depth after $j$ blocks, $D$ bounds each block’s depth, and $\mathcal F_j=\sigma(\omega_0,\ldots,\omega_{j-1})$ records the first $j$ types. In particular, $\sigma_j\le Dj$. The logarithmic-depth bands are $[a_n,B_n]$, where $a_n=\lceil An\rceil-1$, and their expected total weight on $E_n=\{\sigma_n\le A'n\}$ is at least $cn$.

<a id="eq:suffix-convolution"></a>
Let $\theta$ denote the left shift of the type sequence. Define the suffix laws, including their contraction from the initial scale, by $$\tau_j=\mathsf D_{R_j}\mu_{\theta^j\omega},\qquad j\ge0.$$ The independent conditional block draws give the pathwise identity $$\label{eq:suffix-convolution}
 \tau_j=(\mathsf D_{R_j}\nu_{\theta^j\omega,n})*\tau_{j+n},
 \qquad n\ge1.$$ All these laws have compact support. The signs of $R_j$ are retained in the laws; only scale changes use $|R_j|$.

<a id="eq:window-entropy"></a>
<a id="eq:window-monotonicity"></a>
By Lemma <a href="#lem:nonsaturation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:nonsaturation">4</a>, choose $\delta>0$ such that $$\Delta_v(\mu):=1-G_{v,2v}(\mu)\ge\delta\qquad(v>0).$$ Fix an integer $M>4$ with $5/M<\delta/2$. For an integer target depth $L\ge1$, write $r=2^{-L}$ and define $$\label{eq:window-entropy}
 g_j=G_{r/M,Mr}(\tau_j).$$ Here and below $g_j$ depends on $L$ and $\omega$. The ratio of the two window scales is the integer $M^2$. Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-calculus">2</a> and <a href="#eq:suffix-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:suffix-convolution">[eq:suffix-convolution]</a> therefore imply, for every environment, $$\label{eq:window-monotonicity}
 0\le g_j\le2\log M,\qquad g_j\ge g_{j+1}.$$

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### A gain after averaging the unobserved future

An individual conditional tail need not satisfy the nonsaturation bound for $\mu$. To use that bound, we condition only on the types through the end of the finite block segment producing a fair pair. The unobserved future types then average its tail law back to a scaled copy of $\mu$.

<a id="stmt-lem-conditional-gain"></a>
<div id="lem:conditional-gain" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Conditional pair gain). *For all integers $j\ge0$, $n\ge1$, and $L\ge1$, with $g_j$ as in <a href="#eq:window-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:window-entropy">[eq:window-entropy]</a>, <a id="eq:conditional-gain"></a>$$\label{eq:conditional-gain}
 \mathbb E\bigl[g_j-g_{j+n}\mid\mathcal F_{j+n}\bigr]
 \ge \frac{\delta}{2}
 c_{L-\lceil\sigma_j\rceil}(\nu_{\theta^j\omega,n}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conditional-gain"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-gain</summary>

*Proof.* Condition on $\mathcal F_{j+n}$ and put $\ell=L-\lceil\sigma_j\rceil$. The finite law $\nu_{\theta^j\omega,n}$, the signed products $R_j,R_{j+n}$, and $\ell$ are now fixed. Choose a fair-pair decomposition attaining $c_\ell(\nu_{\theta^j\omega,n})$. Such a choice is measurable in $\mathcal F_{j+n}$: for fixed $j,n,L$ there are only finitely many initial type sequences, so one may fix a maximizer for each of them.

A selected pair of distance $u\in[2^{-\ell},2^{1-\ell})$ has distance $v=|R_j|u$ after scaling. Since $r=2^{-L}$, $$\frac vr\in
 \bigl[2^{\lceil\sigma_j\rceil-\sigma_j},
       2^{1+\lceil\sigma_j\rceil-\sigma_j}\bigr)
 \subset[1,4).$$ Thus $r/M<v<Mr$ and $$\frac{r/M}{v}+\frac{v}{Mr}\le\frac5M.$$ Even if $R_j$ is negative, the scaled pair, after ordering its endpoints, is a translate of the fair law on $\{0,v\}$. Translation invariance and Lemma <a href="#lem:two-point" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:two-point">5</a> show that this pair increases the window entropy of $\tau_{j+n}$ by at least $$\Delta_v(\tau_{j+n})-\frac5M.$$

The types beginning at index $j+n$ are independent of $\mathcal F_{j+n}$. Since averaging $\mu_\omega$ over the environment gives $\mu$, their conditional mean is the identity of measures $$\mathbb E[\tau_{j+n}\mid\mathcal F_{j+n}]
   =\mathsf D_{R_{j+n}}\mu.$$ The functional $\Delta_v=1-G_{v,2v}$ is convex by Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-calculus">2</a>. Each chosen distance $v$ is fixed under the present conditioning. Hence Jensen’s inequality and signed scaling give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[\Delta_v(\tau_{j+n})\mid\mathcal F_{j+n}]
 &\ge\Delta_v(\mathsf D_{R_{j+n}}\mu)\\
 &=\Delta_{v/|R_{j+n}|}(\mu)\ge\delta.
\end{aligned}$$

Apply concavity of $G_{r/M,Mr}$ to the selected pairs and the residual positive measure in the prefix law. If the residual mass is nonzero, normalize it to a probability law; convolution with that law contributes a nonnegative gain by Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-calculus">2</a>, since the scale ratio is $M^2$. The same statement is vacuous when the residual mass is zero. Summing the conditional lower bounds for all the selected pairs therefore gives $$\mathbb E[g_j-g_{j+n}\mid\mathcal F_{j+n}]
 \ge\left(\delta-\frac5M\right)
       c_\ell(\nu_{\theta^j\omega,n})
 \ge\frac\delta2c_\ell(\nu_{\theta^j\omega,n}),$$ as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Choosing disjoint block windows

<a id="eq:separated-bands"></a>
Fix any positive integer $q$. Choose sufficiently large integers $n_1<\cdots<n_q$ successively so that Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-bands" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-bands">9</a> applies, $a_{n_i}\ge1$, and $$\label{eq:separated-bands}
 a_{n_i}-A'n_i-1>B_{n_h}\qquad(h<i).$$ This is possible because the previously chosen $B_{n_h}$ are finite and $$a_n-A'n-1=\lceil An\rceil-A'n-2
 \ge(A-A')n-2\longrightarrow\infty.$$ In particular, the choice uses no upper bound on how fast $B_n$ grows. Call $[a_{n_i},B_{n_i}]$ band $i$. Larger $i$ means finer physical scales, since <a href="#eq:separated-bands" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separated-bands">[eq:separated-bands]</a> gives $a_{n_i}>B_{n_h}$ for $h<i$.

<a id="eq:retention"></a>
Choose an even integer $T$ with <a id="eq:target-range"></a>$$\label{eq:target-range}
 \frac T2>\max_{1\le i\le q}B_{n_i}.$$ For band $i$, use the candidate starts <a id="eq:candidate-starts"></a>$$\label{eq:candidate-starts}
 J_i=\left\{kn_i:k\in\mathbb Z,\ 0\le k\le
                  \left\lfloor\frac{T}{2Dn_i}\right\rfloor\right\}.$$ For each target $L\in\{1,\ldots,T\}$, retain the window $[j,j+n_i)$, $j\in J_i$, exactly on the event $$\label{eq:retention}
 \mathcal A_{i,j,L}=
 \left\{\sigma_{j+n_i}-\sigma_j\le A'n_i,\quad
 a_{n_i}\le L-\lceil\sigma_j\rceil\le B_{n_i}\right\}.$$ This event belongs to $\mathcal F_{j+n_i}$, the same sigma-algebra as the conditioning in Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-gain">10</a>.

The reason for the separation condition is simple. As a block start $j$ moves forward, the adjusted target depth $L-\lceil\sigma_j\rceil$ is nonincreasing. A retained window in a finer band cannot decrease this depth far enough to reach any coarser band. The next lemma makes this order precise for every environment.

<a id="stmt-lem-disjoint-windows"></a>
<div id="lem:disjoint-windows" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:telescoping-windows"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Disjoint retained windows). *For each fixed environment and target $L\in\{1,\ldots,T\}$, all the retained windows in <a href="#eq:retention" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:retention">[eq:retention]</a> are pairwise disjoint. Consequently, $$\label{eq:telescoping-windows}
 \sum_{i=1}^q\sum_{j\in J_i}
 \mathbf1_{\mathcal A_{i,j,L}}(g_j-g_{j+n_i})
 \le2\log M.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-disjoint-windows"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-disjoint-windows</summary>

<a id="eq:window-end-depth"></a>
*Proof.* Set $x_j=L-\lceil\sigma_j\rceil$; this is nonincreasing in $j$. If the band-$i$ window $[j,j+n_i)$ is retained, then $$\lceil\sigma_{j+n_i}\rceil-\lceil\sigma_j\rceil
 \le\sigma_{j+n_i}-\sigma_j+1\le A'n_i+1,$$ so $$\label{eq:window-end-depth}
 x_{j+n_i}\ge a_{n_i}-A'n_i-1.$$ If $[k,k+n_h)$ is a retained window in a coarser band $h<i$, then $x_k\le B_{n_h}$. By <a href="#eq:separated-bands" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separated-bands">[eq:separated-bands]</a> and <a href="#eq:window-end-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:window-end-depth">[eq:window-end-depth]</a>, $$x_{j+n_i}>B_{n_h}\ge x_k.$$ Monotonicity of $x_j$ forces $k>j+n_i$. Thus the window from the finer band ends before the window from the coarser band starts. Within a single band, distinct starts are distinct multiples of its length $n_i$, so its half-open windows also do not overlap.

List the retained windows in increasing order as $[u_1,v_1),\ldots,[u_N,v_N)$, with $v_t\le u_{t+1}$. By <a href="#eq:window-monotonicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:window-monotonicity">[eq:window-monotonicity]</a>, $$\sum_{t=1}^N(g_{u_t}-g_{v_t})
 \le g_{u_1}-g_{v_N}\le2\log M.$$ The empty sum is zero. This proves <a href="#eq:telescoping-windows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:telescoping-windows">[eq:telescoping-windows]</a>. ◻

</details>

Figure <a href="#fig:disjoint-windows" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:disjoint-windows">1</a> shows the order of retained windows from two different bands in this proof.

<figure id="fig:disjoint-windows">

<figcaption>At a fixed target depth, a retained window from a finer band ends before any retained window from a coarser band begins. Here $x_{j} = L − ⌈σ_{j}⌉$ is nonincreasing in the block index. The schematic is not to scale.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-disjoint-windows"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:disjoint-windows`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.86cm,y=0.86cm,>=stealth]
 \draw[->] (0,0)--(12.7,0) node[right] {block index};
 \draw[line width=1.5pt] (1,0)--(5,0);
 \draw[line width=1.5pt] (8,0)--(10.5,0);
 \foreach \x in {1,5,8,10.5}{\draw (\x,-0.12)--(\x,0.12);}
 \node[below] at (1,-0.12) {$j$};
 \node[below] at (5,-0.12) {$j+n_i$};
 \node[below] at (8,-0.12) {$k$};
 \node[below] at (10.5,-0.12) {$k+n_h$};
 \node[above] at (3,0.15) {band $i$};
 \node[above] at (9.25,0.15) {band $h<i$};
 \draw[<->] (5,0.8)--(8,0.8);
 \node[above] at (6.5,0.8) {$x_{j+n_i}>B_{n_h}\ge x_k$};

```

Visual witness description of PDF page 18 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal axis with an arrow on the right end labeled "block index". Four vertical tick marks along the axis mark positions labeled $j$, $j + n_i$, $k$, and $k + n_h$. Above the interval from $j$ to $j + n_i$, a bracket is labeled "band $i$". Above the interval from $k$ to $k + n_h$, a bracket is labeled "band $h < i$". Above the axis between $j + n_i$ and $k$, the relation $x_{j+n_i} > B_{n_h} \ge x_k$ is displayed.
```


<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Summing over target depths

The disjointness lemma bounds the total gain at one physical scale. Summing over targets recovers every pair scale in each band. Indeed, for a candidate $j\in J_i$, $$0\le\sigma_j\le Dj\le T/2.$$ Because $T$ is even, this also gives $0\le\lceil\sigma_j\rceil\le T/2$. Each integer $\ell\in[a_{n_i},B_{n_i}]$ therefore occurs exactly once as $L-\lceil\sigma_j\rceil$ for $1\le L\le T$: take $L=\ell+\lceil\sigma_j\rceil$. Its lower bound is $1$, and its upper bound is at most $B_{n_i}+T/2<T$.

<a id="eq:final-window-count"></a>
Each band has at least $T/(2Dn_i)$ candidate starts, and each contributes expected pair mass at least $cn_i$ after summing the targets. The block length cancels, so every band supplies a fixed multiple of $T$. Take expectations in <a href="#eq:telescoping-windows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:telescoping-windows">[eq:telescoping-windows]</a>. Since the retention indicator is $\mathcal F_{j+n_i}$-measurable, the tower property and Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-gain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-gain">10</a> apply to each summand. Sum the result over $L=1,\ldots,T$ and use the preceding exact correspondence to obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 2T\log M
 &\ge\frac\delta2\sum_{i=1}^q\sum_{j\in J_i}
 \mathbb E\left[
 \mathbf1_{E_{n_i}}(\theta^j\omega)
 \sum_{\ell=a_{n_i}}^{B_{n_i}}
 c_\ell(\nu_{\theta^j\omega,n_i})\right]
 \notag\\
 &\ge\frac{\delta c}{2}\sum_{i=1}^q |J_i|n_i
 \ge\frac{\delta c qT}{4D}.
 \label{eq:final-window-count}
\end{aligned}$$ The second inequality uses Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-bands" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-bands">9</a> and the identical distribution of the type environment under $\theta^j$; independence between different candidate windows is not needed. The last inequality follows from the exact count $$|J_i|=\left\lfloor\frac{T}{2Dn_i}\right\rfloor+1
 \ge\frac{T}{2Dn_i}.$$ After canceling $T$, <a href="#eq:final-window-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-window-count">[eq:final-window-count]</a> bounds the arbitrary integer $q$ by $8D\log M/(\delta c)$. All the constants in this bound were fixed before $q$ was chosen, so this is impossible.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-disjoint-windows-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-disjoint-windows</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* The standard upper bound gives $\dim_H\mu\le\min\{1,h/\chi\}$. If the inequality were strict, then $d=\dim_H\mu$ would satisfy $d<1$ and $d\chi<h$. Section <a href="#sec:types" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:types">4</a> would supply the pair bands used above, and <a href="#eq:final-window-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-window-count">[eq:final-window-count]</a> would give a contradiction. Therefore $\dim_H\mu=\min\{1,h/\chi\}$ for the prescribed positive vector $p$. Since $p$ was arbitrary, the conclusion holds for every such vector, including the cases $h=\chi$ and $h>\chi$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:corollaries"></a>
### Overlap conventions and dimension of the attractor

We first reconcile the two usual word-length conventions for an exact overlap. The following observation also appears in (Hochman 2014, footnote 3) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Hochman2014](#ref-Hochman2014)]. The nonzero contraction hypothesis is useful here as well as in the proof of the measure theorem.

<a id="stmt-lem-overlap-lengths"></a>
<div id="lem:overlap-lengths" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12**. *For a finite family of maps $x\mapsto r_i x+t_i$ with $0<|r_i|<1$, an equality of maps for two distinct nonempty words of arbitrary lengths implies such an equality for two distinct words of the same length.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-overlap-lengths"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-overlap-lengths</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose $\varphi_u=\varphi_v$ and $u\ne v$. If $u$ were a proper prefix of $v$, writing $v=uw$ and cancelling the invertible map $\varphi_u$ would give $\varphi_w=\mathrm{id}$. This is impossible because a nonempty word has absolute contraction less than one. The same applies with the words interchanged. Now $\varphi_{uv}=\varphi_u\circ\varphi_v
=\varphi_v\circ\varphi_u=\varphi_{vu}$. The words $uv$ and $vu$ have the same length and are distinct: if they were equal, their first $\min\{|u|,|v|\}$ symbols would show that either $u=v$ or the shorter word is a proper prefix of the longer. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-no-overlaps"></a>
<div id="cor:no-overlaps" class="corollary">

**Corollary 13** (No exact overlaps). *Let $\Phi$ be a finite nonempty indexed family of real similarities with $0<|r_i|<1$ and no exact overlaps. For every strictly positive probability vector $p$, $$\dim_H\mu_{\Phi,p}=\min\{1,H(p)/\chi(\Phi,p)\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-no-overlaps"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-no-overlaps</summary>

*Proof.* At every length $n$, distinct words give distinct complete maps. Hence $G_n$ is an injective function of its symbol word, so $H(G_n)=nH(p)$ and $h_{\mathrm{RW}}=H(p)$. Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. ◻

</details>

Let $K_\Phi$ be the compact attractor, equivalently the set of all coding limits. Its similarity dimension $s_*$ is the unique number $s_*\ge0$ satisfying $$\sum_{i\in\Lambda}|r_i|^{s_*}=1.$$ For at least two symbols, existence and uniqueness follow because this sum is continuous and strictly decreasing from $|\Lambda|>1$ to zero. For one symbol its unique solution is zero.

<a id="stmt-cor-set"></a>
<div id="cor:set" class="corollary">

**Corollary 14**. *For a finite nonempty family of similarities of $\mathbb R$ with arbitrary real translations and $0<|r_i|<1$, absence of exact overlaps implies $$\dim_H K_\Phi=\min\{1,s_*\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-set"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-set</summary>

*Proof.* For one map the attractor is its fixed point and $s_*=0$. For at least two maps choose the strictly positive weights $p_i=|r_i|^{s_*}$. Then $H(p)=s_*\chi(\Phi,p)$. Corollary <a href="#cor:no-overlaps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:no-overlaps">13</a> gives a measure supported on $K_\Phi$ of dimension $\min\{1,s_*\}$, which is a lower bound for its set dimension. For the upper bound, if $a>s_*$, the level-$n$ cylinder sets cover $K_\Phi$, their diameters tend uniformly to zero, and $$\sum_{u\in\Lambda^n}(\operatorname{diam}\varphi_u(K_\Phi))^a
 = (\operatorname{diam}K_\Phi)^a
   \left(\sum_i|r_i|^a\right)^n\longrightarrow0.$$ The zero-diameter case is immediate. Thus $\dim_H K_\Phi\le s_*$, and the ambient upper bound is one. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-homogeneous"></a>
<div id="cor:homogeneous" class="corollary">

**Corollary 15**. *Let $m\ge2$, $0<\lambda<1$, and $t_1,\ldots,t_m\in\mathbb R$. If the maps $x\mapsto\lambda x+t_i$ have no exact overlaps, then for every strictly positive probability vector $p$ their self-similar measure has $$\dim_H\mu=\min\left\{1,\frac{H(p)}{\log(1/\lambda)}\right\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-homogeneous"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-homogeneous</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Corollary <a href="#cor:no-overlaps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:no-overlaps">13</a> with $r_i=\lambda$ for every $i$. Since the weights sum to one, $\chi=\log(1/\lambda)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-three-maps"></a>
<div id="cor:three-maps" class="corollary">

**Corollary 16** (Three maps). *Let $0<\lambda<1$ and $t\in\mathbb R$. If the indexed maps $$x\longmapsto\lambda x,\qquad
 x\longmapsto\lambda x+1,\qquad
 x\longmapsto\lambda x+t$$ have no exact overlaps, then for every $p_0,p_1,p_2>0$ with $p_0+p_1+p_2=1$, their self-similar measure satisfies $$\dim_H\mu
 =\min\left\{1,\frac{-\sum_{i=0}^2 p_i\log p_i}
                         {\log(1/\lambda)}\right\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-three-maps"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-three-maps</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Corollary <a href="#cor:homogeneous" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:homogeneous">15</a> with translations $0,1,t$. ◻

</details>

No claim of absolute continuity, effective separation, or a quantitative rate of convergence follows from these dimension statements. In particular, the finite upper endpoints of the scale ranges used in the proof have no asserted effective bound.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "At a fixed target depth, a retained window from a finer band ends before any retained window from a coarser band begins. Here $x_{j} = L − ⌈σ_{j}⌉$ is nonincreasing in the block index. The schematic is not to scale.",
      "figure_id": "fig:disjoint-windows",
      "pdf_page": 18,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:disjoint-windows",
          "exact_quote": "At a fixed target depth, a retained window from a finer band\nends before any retained window from a coarser band begins.  Here\n$x_j=L-\\lceil\\sigma_j\\rceil$ is nonincreasing in the block index.  The\nschematic is not to scale.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.86cm,y=0.86cm,>=stealth]\n \\draw[->] (0,0)--(12.7,0) node[right] {block index};\n \\draw[line width=1.5pt] (1,0)--(5,0);\n \\draw[line width=1.5pt] (8,0)--(10.5,0);\n \\foreach \\x in {1,5,8,10.5}{\\draw (\\x,-0.12)--(\\x,0.12);}\n \\node[below] at (1,-0.12) {$j$};\n \\node[below] at (5,-0.12) {$j+n_i$};\n \\node[below] at (8,-0.12) {$k$};\n \\node[below] at (10.5,-0.12) {$k+n_h$};\n \\node[above] at (3,0.15) {band $i$};\n \\node[above] at (9.25,0.15) {band $h<i$};\n \\draw[<->] (5,0.8)--(8,0.8);\n \\node[above] at (6.5,0.8) {$x_{j+n_i}>B_{n_h}\\ge x_k$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A horizontal axis with an arrow on the right end labeled \"block index\". Four vertical tick marks along the axis mark positions labeled $j$, $j + n_i$, $k$, and $k + n_h$. Above the interval from $j$ to $j + n_i$, a bracket is labeled \"band $i$\". Above the interval from $k$ to $k + n_h$, a bracket is labeled \"band $h < i$\". Above the axis between $j + n_i$ and $k$, the relation $x_{j+n_i} > B_{n_h} \\ge x_k$ is displayed."
    }
  ],
